I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.

Then I look at it and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nI mean, I’m trying, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been getting embarrassing. I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. People have been tense with me a few times, and I don’t blame them exactly, but it makes me feel awful.\n\nAnd even outside work, at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead some planning things because I could tell people were getting frustrated. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but yes… it does make me anxious and embarrassed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Then I look at it and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nI mean, I’m trying, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been getting embarrassing. I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. People have been tense with me a few times, and I don’t blame them exactly, but it makes me feel awful.\n\nAnd even outside work, at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead some planning things because I could tell people were getting frustrated. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but yes… it does make me anxious and embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing similar difficulties with organization and planning in your personal life, outside of work and church?\n\nPatient: Well… outside of work and church, I’m not sure it’s as obvious. I mean, the main thing that made me come in is really the work stuff—tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer orders all ending up together until I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nAt home I suppose I have my piles too, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal busy-life clutter. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Organization_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is I cannot seem to keep my work in order anymore, with tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs all piled together until I do not know what to handle first.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.

What really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.

Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a harder time keeping things in order at work. I do laundry and dry cleaning, and lately the tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs—everything kind of ends up piled together. Then I look at it and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nI mean, I’m trying, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been getting embarrassing. I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. People have been tense with me a few times, and I don’t blame them exactly, but it makes me feel awful.\n\nAnd even outside work, at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead some planning things because I could tell people were getting frustrated. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but yes… it does make me anxious and embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing similar difficulties with organization and planning in your personal life, outside of work and church?\n\nPatient: Well… outside of work and church, I’m not sure it’s as obvious. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.

I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.

I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.

But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. Not because I don’t care, but because I could tell others were getting irritated when I wasn’t keeping things organized the way they expected. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself tapping your hands or feet or engaging in other fidgety behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that. I mean, I might shift around in my chair or move my hands a little, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big thing if I’m not sure.\n\nI’m not usually thinking, “Oh, I’m tapping,” you know? I think it’s maybe more the staying with the details, if that makes sense. I can start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I mean to be careful with it.

Then I look at it and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nI mean, I’m trying, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been getting embarrassing. I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. People have been tense with me a few times, and I don’t blame them exactly, but it makes me feel awful.\n\nAnd even outside work, at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead some planning things because I could tell people were getting frustrated. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but yes… it does make me anxious and embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing similar difficulties with organization and planning in your personal life, outside of work and church?\n\nPatient: Well… outside of work and church, I’m not sure it’s as obvious. I mean, the main thing that made me come in is really the work stuff—tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer orders all ending up together until I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nAt home I suppose I have my piles too, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal busy-life clutter. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. Not because I don’t care, but because I could tell others were getting irritated when I wasn’t keeping things organized the way they expected. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself tapping your hands or feet or engaging in other fidgety behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that. I mean, I might shift around in my chair or move my hands a little, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big thing if I’m not sure.\n\nI’m not usually thinking, “Oh, I’m tapping,” you know? If someone else noticed it, they might tell me better than I could. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are more related to paying attention to details or to remembering what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s maybe more the staying with the details, if that makes sense. I can start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I mean to be careful with it.\n\nBut after a few minutes, my mind kind of drifts—to the labels, or something with choir, or something I need to do at home—and then I’ll realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. So… it may look like I forgot, but sometimes I think I just didn’t stay focused long enough in the first place. Is that answering it the right way?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F98.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Is that answering it the right way?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your mind wandering off to other things when you're supposed to be focused is more of a problem for you than forgetting what you were supposed to do in the first place?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I can start going over the day's dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is off on labels, choir plans, or something at home, and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I usually do know what I’m supposed to be doing, like going through the dry-cleaning orders. I can start it okay and I mean to do it carefully.

But then after a few minutes, my mind just sort of goes somewhere else — the labels, or choir plans, or something at home — and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.

So I guess it’s more the staying focused than not knowing what I was supposed to do. If that makes sense.

I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. Yes… I think I might, even if I really wanted to do it right. Not because I don’t care.

I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. I mean, the main thing that made me come in is really the work stuff—tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer orders all ending up together until I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nAt home I suppose I have my piles too, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal busy-life clutter. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. Not because I don’t care, but because I could tell others were getting irritated when I wasn’t keeping things organized the way they expected. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself tapping your hands or feet or engaging in other fidgety behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that. I mean, I might shift around in my chair or move my hands a little, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big thing if I’m not sure.\n\nI’m not usually thinking, “Oh, I’m tapping,” you know? If someone else noticed it, they might tell me better than I could. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are more related to paying attention to details or to remembering what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s maybe more the staying with the details, if that makes sense. I can start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I mean to be careful with it.\n\nBut after a few minutes, my mind kind of drifts—to the labels, or something with choir, or something I need to do at home—and then I’ll realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. So… it may look like I forgot, but sometimes I think I just didn’t stay focused long enough in the first place. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering off to other things when you're supposed to be focused is more of a problem for you than forgetting what you were supposed to do in the first place?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I usually do know what I’m supposed to be doing, like going through the dry-cleaning orders. I can start it okay and I mean to do it carefully.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, my mind just sort of goes somewhere else — the labels, or choir plans, or something at home — and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.\n\nSo I guess it’s more the staying focused than not knowing what I was supposed to do. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have difficulty following a set of instructions or a recipe even if you were highly motivated to do so?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might, even if I really wanted to do it right. Not because I don’t care.\n\nLike at work, if someone gives me three things at once — call the supplier, tag the silk blouse, and enter the receipt — I’ll usually do the first thing, and then something else catches my attention or someone asks me something, and I realize later I didn’t finish the rest.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed saying that, because I do try. But yes, I think following through on all the steps can be hard for me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are the main reasons you're having trouble at work, or are there other factors contributing to your stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think the organization and focus are a big part of it, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like everything at work is my fault, but… there have been late orders and some billing mix-ups, and then that makes people tense — staff and customers both.\n\nAnd at church, too, I’ve kind of stepped back from leading some of the planning, because I could tell people were getting frustrated with me. I hate admitting that. So yes, I think those difficulties are causing a lot of the stress, though the stress probably makes it feel worse too. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. Not because I don’t care, but because I could tell others were getting irritated when I wasn’t keeping things organized the way they expected. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself tapping your hands or feet or engaging in other fidgety behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that. I mean, I might shift around in my chair or move my hands a little, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big thing if I’m not sure.\n\nI’m not usually thinking, “Oh, I’m tapping,” you know? If someone else noticed it, they might tell me better than I could. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are more related to paying attention to details or to remembering what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s maybe more the staying with the details, if that makes sense. I can start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I mean to be careful with it.\n\nBut after a few minutes, my mind kind of drifts—to the labels, or something with choir, or something I need to do at home—and then I’ll realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. So… it may look like I forgot, but sometimes I think I just didn’t stay focused long enough in the first place. I usually do know what I’m supposed to be doing, like going through the dry-cleaning orders. I can start it okay and I mean to do it carefully.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, my mind just sort of goes somewhere else — the labels, or choir plans, or something at home — and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.\n\nSo I guess it’s more the staying focused than not knowing what I was supposed to do. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have difficulty following a set of instructions or a recipe even if you were highly motivated to do so?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might, even if I really wanted to do it right. Not because I don’t care.\n\nLike at work, if someone gives me three things at once — call the supplier, tag the silk blouse, and enter the receipt — I’ll usually do the first thing, and then something else catches my attention or someone asks me something, and I realize later I didn’t finish the rest.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed saying that, because I do try. But yes, I think following through on all the steps can be hard for me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are the main reasons you're having trouble at work, or are there other factors contributing to your stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think the organization and focus are a big part of it, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like everything at work is my fault, but… there have been late orders and some billing mix-ups, and then that makes people tense — staff and customers both.\n\nAnd at church, too, I’ve kind of stepped back from leading some of the planning, because I could tell people were getting frustrated with me. I hate admitting that. So yes, I think those difficulties are causing a lot of the stress, though the stress probably makes it feel worse too. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever been diagnosed with that. I mean, not formally or anything.\n\nI may have wondered a little when I’ve heard people talk about it, but I always thought maybe I was just… disorganized, or trying to do too much. I didn’t want to make excuses. But no, no one has ever told me that’s what it was. Is that something you’re considering?\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me more about what you experience when you try to sit still and focus on a task, like reading or listening to someone speak?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I can start going over the day's dry-cleaning orders just fine, but after a few minutes my mind is off on labels, choir plans, or something at home, and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I can sit there and try, and at first I’m usually okay. Like if I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I’ll start out checking them carefully.

But after a few minutes, my mind kind of… wanders off. I’ll be thinking about a label on something, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home, and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.

It’s not that I’m trying to ignore it. I do want to pay attention. It just slips away before I notice, if that makes sense.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a harder time keeping things in order at work. I do laundry and dry cleaning, and lately the tickets, receipts, notes from the workshop, customer jobs—everything kind of ends up piled together. Then I look at it and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nI mean, I’m trying, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been getting embarrassing. I just thought maybe I should ask someone about it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How much distress or anxiety are you experiencing because of this disorganization at work?\n\nPatient: I guess… more than I’d like to admit. I try not to get too worked up about it, because everyone makes mistakes, but it has been stressful.\n\nThere have been late orders at the shop because I didn’t keep things straight, and sometimes the billing gets mixed up, so then I have to explain it to a customer or one of the other staff. People have been tense with me a few times, and I don’t blame them exactly, but it makes me feel awful.\n\nAnd even outside work, at church, I’ve kind of stepped back from helping lead some planning things because I could tell people were getting frustrated. I don’t know if that answers what you mean, but yes… it does make me anxious and embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing similar difficulties with organization and planning in your personal life, outside of work and church?\n\nPatient: Well… outside of work and church, I’m not sure it’s as obvious. I mean, the main thing that made me come in is really the work stuff—tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer orders all ending up together until I don’t know what I’m supposed to do first.\n\nAt home I suppose I have my piles too, but I’ve been telling myself that’s just normal busy-life clutter. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more that at work it’s gotten hard to keep things in order in a way that affects other people, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble keeping things organized at home when you're not stressed or under a deadline?\n\nPatient: Um… at home, I guess I haven’t thought of it the same way. It’s not as serious there, or at least it doesn’t feel like it causes as many problems.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that at work I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore. The tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all end up piled together, and then I look at it and don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first. I’m sorry if I’m not answering exactly, but that’s the part that’s been most noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any areas in your life where you feel you are highly organized and in control?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say yes… and I’m sure there are little things I manage okay. But honestly, the place I’m not feeling in control is work.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. I can’t seem to keep the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, and customer jobs straight anymore. They all end up together, and then I’m standing there looking at the pile not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first. I try to make it look more organized than it is, but it catches up with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to organize your work or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Not every second, but it does happen.\n\nLike, I’ll start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders and I’m fine at first, really trying to check them properly. Then a few minutes later my mind has wandered off to the labels, or something I need to do for choir, or something at home. And then I sort of come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of distracted you mean, but yes, that does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m reckless or anything… I don’t think I do big impulsive things. But I do sometimes jump in too fast when someone’s talking.\n\nLike my daughter will be asking me something, or a customer, or even the trainer at work, and I’ll answer before they’ve finished the question. Then I realize I didn’t actually hear the whole thing, and I answered the wrong part. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t mean to interrupt people… I just kind of jump ahead. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with organization and planning, or is this a relatively new challenge for you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly… though I don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything. I did okay in some ways.\n\nBut even in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but kind of scattered. I’d talk out of turn, lose papers, need reminders to finish my work… things like that. So I guess it isn’t completely new. It’s just harder to cover up now, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly bouncing around or anything like that.\n\nIf I’m sitting for a while, I might shift around or play with my hands a little, but I don’t know if that’s more than normal. I haven’t really paid attention to it, honestly. Is that what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble focusing on tasks or activities when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not all the time, but it does happen.\n\nEven at home, if I’m trying to sit down and go over the day’s dry-cleaning orders or check something I brought with me, I can start out fine. Then after a few minutes my mind is suddenly on the clothing labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home… and then I realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nI try to bring myself back to it, but it’s a little embarrassing. I don’t know if that counts as trouble focusing, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be active and have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I feel a need to be active exactly. I mean, I do like to keep busy, but I always thought that was just normal… or maybe just how my days are.\n\nFor quiet things, I can sit for a little while, but I guess I don’t really choose quiet leisure that often. I’m not sure if that’s because I have trouble with it or because I just don’t think to do it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings or extreme emotional reactions to everyday situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I wouldn’t call it intense or extreme.\n\nMaybe I get frustrated sometimes if something doesn’t go right, but I try not to make a big deal out of it. I haven’t really noticed big mood swings, at least not in a way that seems unusual. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with family or friends due to your difficulties with organization and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t like to say it that way, because I really do try to keep things running smoothly.\n\nAt the shop, there have been times orders were late, or billing got mixed up, and that can make things tense with the staff or customers. I usually try to fix it and smooth it over, but I can tell people get frustrated with me sometimes.\n\nAnd at church… I’ve stepped back from leading some of the planning. Not because I don’t care, but because I could tell others were getting irritated when I wasn’t keeping things organized the way they expected. I hope that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself tapping your hands or feet or engaging in other fidgety behaviors when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that. I mean, I might shift around in my chair or move my hands a little, but I wouldn’t want to say it’s a big thing if I’m not sure.\n\nI’m not usually thinking, “Oh, I’m tapping,” you know? If someone else noticed it, they might tell me better than I could. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are more related to paying attention to details or to remembering what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I think it’s maybe more the staying with the details, if that makes sense. I can start going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders just fine, and I mean to be careful with it.\n\nBut after a few minutes, my mind kind of drifts—to the labels, or something with choir, or something I need to do at home—and then I’ll realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. So… it may look like I forgot, but sometimes I think I just didn’t stay focused long enough in the first place. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wandering off to other things when you're supposed to be focused is more of a problem for you than forgetting what you were supposed to do in the first place?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. I usually do know what I’m supposed to be doing, like going through the dry-cleaning orders. I can start it okay and I mean to do it carefully.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, my mind just sort of goes somewhere else — the labels, or choir plans, or something at home — and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check.\n\nSo I guess it’s more the staying focused than not knowing what I was supposed to do. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have difficulty following a set of instructions or a recipe even if you were highly motivated to do so?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I might, even if I really wanted to do it right. Not because I don’t care.\n\nLike at work, if someone gives me three things at once — call the supplier, tag the silk blouse, and enter the receipt — I’ll usually do the first thing, and then something else catches my attention or someone asks me something, and I realize later I didn’t finish the rest.\n\nI’m a little embarrassed saying that, because I do try. But yes, I think following through on all the steps can be hard for me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with organization and staying focused are the main reasons you're having trouble at work, or are there other factors contributing to your stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think the organization and focus are a big part of it, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like everything at work is my fault, but… there have been late orders and some billing mix-ups, and then that makes people tense — staff and customers both.\n\nAnd at church, too, I’ve kind of stepped back from leading some of the planning, because I could tell people were getting frustrated with me. I hate admitting that. So yes, I think those difficulties are causing a lot of the stress, though the stress probably makes it feel worse too. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve ever been diagnosed with that. I mean, not formally or anything.\n\nI may have wondered a little when I’ve heard people talk about it, but I always thought maybe I was just… disorganized, or trying to do too much. I didn’t want to make excuses. But no, no one has ever told me that’s what it was. Is that something you’re considering?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you experience when you try to sit still and focus on a task, like reading or listening to someone speak?\n\nPatient: I can sit there and try, and at first I’m usually okay. Like if I’m going over the day’s dry-cleaning orders, I’ll start out checking them carefully.\n\nBut after a few minutes, my mind kind of… wanders off. I’ll be thinking about a label on something, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home, and then I look back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to ignore it. I do want to pay attention. It just slips away before I notice, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
